# Preface.

The following treatise is found among the academic dissertations of the famous Morhof <note>Daniel Georg Morhof (1639–1691) was a towering figure in German scholarship, best known for his work "Polyhistor," which attempted to categorize all human knowledge.</note>, which were published in quarto <note>The source text uses "in 4.", referring to a book size where sheets are folded twice to create four leaves.</note> in Hamburg in 1699, and have since become somewhat rare. Anyone who knows the great merits of this man—who was learned in all branches of the sciences—will not be surprised that he ventured into this field, in which he demonstrated no small degree of mastery.

## Physico-Historical Treatise
->on the<-
# Transformation of Metals.

It is a reasonable question to ask whether metals can be transformed. Since such a transformation necessarily requires the destruction of one metal and the creation of another, it is first and foremost necessary to investigate how Nature assembles such metals within the bowels <note>original: "Eingeweiden der Erde"; a common early modern metaphor suggesting that minerals and metals "grew" inside the earth like organic life.</note> of the earth. Yet who would not confess their ignorance here? Or who can construct a clear system of knowledge <term>Lehrgebäude: a structured, comprehensive theoretical framework</term> regarding this matter, given the darkness in which it remains hidden? For many centuries, no one has yet emerged who could reliably demonstrate or reveal the fundamental causes of metals, even though we investigate other natural things so precisely that we reach the very "parts of particles" <note>This refers to the 17th-century interest in corpuscular theory or early atomism, where scientists attempted to understand matter by its smallest physical components.</note>. In this

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the treatise, posing the fundamental question of whether metals can be transmuted and noting that such a feat requires a deep understanding of how metals are naturally formed within the earth.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, transmutation, alchemy, natural philosophy, corpuscular theory, 17th century science</keywords>

matter, we have had nothing until now but conjectures; we grope in the dark; nowhere is there anything certain, and what we know of the nature of metals has only arisen from a shallow investigation. Nature has perhaps hidden their primary matter <term>Urstoff: the original or fundamental substance from which all metals are thought to be formed</term> in such a way that the efforts of mortals will hardly uncover it. Her operations, which she employs, are slow and sometimes extend through whole centuries, and indeed in such a way that they easily escape the diligence of even the most attentive observer. Metallurgists are unlearned <note>German: *Ungelehrte*. The author is likely distinguishing between practical miners/smelters and theoretical scholars.</note> and possess no scientific knowledge of the causes of natural things. The philosophers <term>Weltweisen: literally "world-wise ones," referring to natural philosophers or scientists of the era</term>, who could investigate such things with greater sagacity, do not wish to expose themselves to the danger <note>This likely refers to the physical hazards of mining and chemistry, or perhaps the social and financial risks associated with failed alchemical pursuits.</note> associated with it. And for this reason, the noblest part of natural philosophy <term>Naturlehre: the study of the laws of nature, equivalent to modern physics and chemistry</term> remains uncultivated and desolate, through the neglect of which we also remain uncertain in its other parts. How many and how important are the changes the entire atmosphere suffers from the internal composition of our globe, its parts and exhalations? Among these, the internal salts alongside the metals occupy the most prominent place, acting as it were like the juices and the marrow <note>This reflects the early modern "organicist" view of the earth, where minerals and salts were seen as the vital fluids and structural core of a living planet.</note> of the globe. Certainly, the most significant phenomena depend upon them. Among the authors who write about metals, one will find little comfort. What, however, in this

matter has yet been done, one finds among the Germans, whom foreigners consult when they treat this subject. In the ancient writers, one finds in **Plato**, **Aristotle**, and **Pliny** <note>original: "Plinius"</note> some traces of the history of metals. Among the more recent [authors], following **Albertus Magnus’s** <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and polymath who wrote extensively on minerals.</note> books on minerals, the treatise of **Andreas von Solea** on the growth and decline of metals—which is commonly attributed to **Basilius Valentinus** <note>Basilius Valentinus was a legendary 15th-century alchemist; scholars now believe these works were actually written in the early 1600s by the publisher Johann Thölde.</note> and appears in his *Twelve Keys*—is well written and contains much that is hidden, even though they lived a hundred years apart; yet most of it is obscure and not presented clearly enough. **Georg Agricola** <note>often called the "Father of Mineralogy" for his seminal 1556 work *De Re Metallica*.</note> is a diligent and learned writer who, to the best of his ability, details the history of metals and their effects. To him may be added: **Lazarus Erker’s** <term>Probierkunst: the art of assaying, or testing ore to determine its metal content</term>, likewise **Fuchs**, **Mathesius**, **Albinus**, **Zacharias Theobaldus** (who, however, took everything from others), and **Löhneisen’s** works, all of which are written in German. Also, **Andreas Caesalpinus** <note>original: "Cäsalpinus"; an Italian physician and botanist who classified minerals based on their physical properties.</note> is not to be despised. **Bernhard Casius** says something of it here and there; however, he only has general pronouncements on metallic matters, which [contribute] little to the knowledge of their true nature...

Also not to be forgotten are those who gathered the various fossils <term>Fossilien: In 18th-century natural history, this term referred to any mineral, ore, or substance "dug up" from the earth, not specifically the petrified remains of living things.</term> found in different parts of the world, such as **Kentmann**, **Schwenkfeld**, **Kretschmar**, **Merrettus**, **Aldrovandus**, and **Worm**.

**Athanasius Kircher** <note>A German Jesuit polymath who published *Mundus Subterraneus* (The Subterranean World) in 1665, a work famous for its stunning illustrations but often criticized for its speculative nature.</note> indeed promised magnificent things with great fanfare in his *Subterranean World*: however, one is very much cheated in one’s expectations. As voluminous as this book is, it is nonetheless empty of substance, such that the author not infrequently leaves out the most necessary details, smears together much that is superfluous, reheats old matters, copies much from others, cites many things incorrectly, and very rarely brings anything new to the market.

Much more ingenious is **Becher** <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a German physician and alchemist who was a major influence on early chemistry.</note> in his *Subterranean Physics* <note>original: *Physica Subterranea*</note>, who in this book and its supplements provides much that sheds light on metallic matters. **Honoratus Fabri** and **du Hamel** also deserve their praise; the former in his books on physics, and the latter in his treatise *On Meteors and Fossils* <note>original: *de Meteoris et Fossilibus*</note> have explained this part of natural history quite well.

->§. 2.<-

Those who make **water** the primary substance <term>Urstof: The fundamental, original matter or "first element" from which all physical things are composed.</term> of all bodies also accept it as the foundational material of metals. This opinion of the ancient philosophers has been sought out again by the moderns **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a chemist who famously used a willow tree experiment to argue that all matter is made of water.</note>, **Bernhard Palissy** <note>A French potter and hydraulics engineer (c. 1510–1589) who made early contributions to geology and the study of salts.</note>, and **Heinrich von Rochaz**. This also confirms the opinion of a certain general solvent <note>original: *Auflösungswasser*</note> that many call **Alkahest** <note>In alchemy, the "Alkahest" was a hypothetical universal solvent capable of dissolving any substance, including gold, into its primary liquid form.</note>, through which natural bodies, and thus even the metals themselves, are supposedly reduced to a tasteless **liquid** <note>original: *Liquor*</note>. This hypothesis has many...

...taken up, and many furnaces of the **chemists** <term>Chymisten: Early modern practitioners whose work spanned what we now distinguish as alchemy, pharmacy, and chemistry.</term> have been fired up, but with poor success. For no one has yet been found who could boast of having seen this **Liquor** <note>The author is referring to the *Alkahest*, a legendary universal solvent sought by alchemists, particularly those of the Paracelsian school.</note>, whereas quite a few, as history makes clear, have seen the great **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*; the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and providing an elixir of life.</note> more than once. Indeed, **Helmont** contradicts himself. For in one place he says that through this liquid, bodies would be dissolved into pure and clear water; but in another place he asserts that dregs <term>Feces: In early chemistry, this refers to the solid residue, sediment, or "dross" left over after a substance has been dissolved or distilled.</term> remained behind. Before the times of **Paracelsus** and **Helmontius** <note>The Latinized name for Jan Baptista van Helmont.</note>, such things were not heard of in the schools of the chemists; for the writings of **Villanovanus** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a Catalan physician and alchemist.</note>, **Lullius** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316); though a philosopher, many alchemical texts were attributed to him in the late Middle Ages.</note>, **Bacon** <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), the English friar and philosopher known for his interest in natural science.</note>, and other such philosophers speak of all other matters. If one considers the matter itself, it seems to conflict with the nature of things. For the natures of liquid and solid bodies are distinct; and although solid bodies are sometimes released from the bonds by which they are held together, and then first elude the eyes <note>A poetic way of saying the solid has dissolved into a solution and is no longer visible.</note>, they are nonetheless never transformed into one another, nor do they have their beginning or origin from them. **Bernhard Palissy** <note>c. 1510–1589; a French potter and hydraulic engineer who contributed to early geology and soil science.</note>, a man who was indeed common yet ingenious, asserts in his books written in French concerning the nature of springs, metals, and precious stones:

...that everything, even metals and stones, were produced from water; yet he assumes two kinds of water: a material one and a coagulating one. If one considers this correctly, however, one sees that he assumes two types of **primary matter** <term>Urstof: In early modern science, this refers to the fundamental element or "first matter" from which all physical things are composed.</term> and says nothing new other than using different words. His countryman **Heinrich von Rochaz** <note>Henri de Rochas (17th century), a French physician and mineralogist whose works on mineral waters and mines were influential in early modern chemistry.</note>, who published something regarding metallic waters and the secrets of ore mines and also wrote a ***Physicam reformatam*** <note>original: *Physicam reformatam* (Latin for "Reformed Physics"). This refers to Rochas's work *La Physique reformée* (1648), which sought to reconcile chemical principles with natural philosophy.</note>, indeed also makes water the material primary matter of things; yet he adds **salt** to it, which is his basis for **consolidation** <note>In this context, consolidation refers to the process of a liquid hardening or "fixing" into a solid state.</note>. In doing so, he nevertheless assumes more immediate materials in the composition of bodies.

The excellent **Robert Boyle** <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691) was a founding figure of modern chemistry, though his work on gemstones still blended mechanical philosophy with older alchemical observations.</note> is of the same opinion in his book on **gemstones**, whose original essence he assumes to be liquid; for when they are soft and liquid and mixed with certain mineral juices, they take on their colors as required by the metal present. He believes that in dark stones—for example, **bloodstone** <note>Hematite or a green chalcedony with red spots.</note>, **jasper**, and the like—the metallic earth impregnated with juices coagulates into the form of a stone when a **petrifying juice** <note>original: *versteinernder Saft*. The 17th-century theory of *succus lapidificus* suggested a specific liquid or "spirit" was responsible for turning organic or fluid matter into stone.</note> or **stone-making spirit** is added. There is much of beauty in the book that explains this subject. In it, he mentions a certain thing that happened to come upon—

...expelled **liquids** <term>Liquors: In early modern chemistry, this refers to fluid substances or solutions, often those with dissolving properties.</term> that dissolved gemstones. The earth, he believes, is full of **solvents** <term>Menstruis: From the Latin *menstruum*, a term used by alchemists to describe a solvent or a liquid that can dissolve solids and "nourish" the growth of minerals.</term> and other such juices which, as they run through ore mines, are impregnated in various ways. These serve in some cases as a solvent, but can contribute in other ways to the generation of minerals. Indeed, he believes even common water, which usually carries all sorts of mineral particles (as **Thurneysser** <note>Leonhard Thurneysser (1531–1596), a Swiss-born alchemist and physician to the Elector of Brandenburg, famous for his work on the properties of mineral waters.</note> teaches in his book on mineral waters), can accomplish this. He asserts that he has explained this subject more extensively in his book ***On Subterranean Solvents*** <note>original: *De Menstruis subterraneis*</note>.

While mentioning this, I am reminded of what **Abraham Portaleone** <note>Abraham ben David Portaleone (1542–1612), a prominent Jewish physician from Mantua known for his work *De Auro* (On Gold), which synthesized medical and alchemical knowledge.</note>, a Jewish physician, relates in his treatise on gold regarding its medicinal power. He speaks of the **Favian waters** <note>Referring to specific mineral-rich springs near the Rhine; early naturalists believed these waters contained gold in a pre-solidified, "juice-like" state.</note> on the Rhine, stating that they contain a **juice of gold** that has not yet solidified; this, he says, is the source of their excellent effect on the sick. For in these waters, one sees no actual grains of gold, which are otherwise found in the rivers nearest the borders of the Rhine, and yet those rivers lack this healing power. In the latter, he believes, such juice grows into a physical gold. For this reason, he [recommends] the use of wine in which gold leaf has been quenched/

...rejects it, because such water cannot obtain any power from the gold grains.

Whether all of this is true, I leave for others to judge. In obscure matters that are so far removed from the senses, only conjectures have a place; and if we admit these **subterranean solvents** <term>Menstrua: In early chemistry, "menstrua" (the plural of menstruum) refers to solvents that could dissolve solids. Alchemists believed the earth contained such fluids to "digest" or grow minerals.</term>, it is not done entirely without reason. Something similar was recently put forward by **Thomas Sherley** <note>Thomas Sherley (1638–1678) was a physician to King Charles II of England. His work, *A Philosophical Essay declaring the probable Causes whence Stones are produced*, was published in 1672.</note>, personal physician to the King of England, in his treatise on the causes of stones. On this occasion, he examines the origin of all bodies, which he derives from water and their "seeds." He intends to pave the way for his treatise, which he plans to write on stones in the human body. In it, not a few wonderful things regarding this subject in general and the nature of **petrification** <term>Versteinerung: The process by which organic or liquid matter is transformed into stone.</term> are brought forward, though we cannot dwell upon them here.

Finally, the ingenious **Mr. Steno** <note>Nicolaus Steno (1638–1686) was a Danish pioneer in both anatomy and geology. His 1669 work *De solido* is a foundational text for modern geology, explaining how solid objects like fossils and crystals become embedded in other solids (rock layers).</note> will elaborate on this matter with great care and much diligence in his treatise ***On a Solid Naturally Contained Within a Solid*** <note>original: *de solido intra solidum naturaliter contento*</note>, of which he preliminarily published something last year, if I am not mistaken, from which one can easily judge the future work. A natural body, he says, is either solid or fluid. The former is from a

<summary>This page discusses 17th-century theories on the formation of minerals and fossils, citing the work of Thomas Sherley and the landmark geological studies of Nicolaus Steno.</summary>
<keywords>gold grains, subterranean solvents, Thomas Sherley, petrification, Nicolaus Steno, mineralogy, geology, 17th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicolaus Steno’s geological theories from his 1669 work. The previous page introduced Steno's revolutionary idea that solid bodies like crystals and fossils grow within other solids. This page specifically describes his theory of "accretion"—how solids form from liquids—and the role of "vapors" in mineral formation.</meta>

...from a liquid, if it has arisen according to the rules of nature. It grows by having new particles added to its own, which separate from the external liquids; however, this accretion <term>Ansetzung: The process of particles attaching to a solid surface, a fundamental concept in Steno's theory of crystal growth.</term> occurs either directly from an external liquid or indirectly from several internal liquid substances. All of this is beautifully illustrated by a comparison of the human body with the **products of the earth**. He teaches how one must carefully examine the place of generation, namely the adjacent bodies which are joined together so that they give the produced object its shape. In this way, much more could be discovered in the further investigation of stones that is sought in vain in the investigation of minerals; hence it is often probable that all minerals found within the wide and narrow cracks of stones had a **vapor** <note>original: "Dampf." In 17th-century science, "vapors" or "exhalations" were thought to carry the material essence of minerals through the earth's crust to form ore veins.</note> from the stones themselves as their matter. I will let this stand as it is, for these crevices could also be filled from sources other than from the vapors driven out of the stones themselves.

Regarding the **generation of crystals**, he says much that is astute, and investigates whether they are produced between two liquids, or between a solid and a liquid body, or whether they are produced within the liquid itself. For, that they arise from a liquid body is for him beyond <note>The text cuts off here. The catchword "keinem" at the bottom of the original page suggests the sentence completes as "is for him [subject to] no [doubt].</note>

<vocab>Nature, Products of the Earth, Minerals, Stones, Crystals, Matter</vocab>

<summary>This page details Nicolaus Steno's theory that crystals and minerals grow by the external accretion of particles from liquids or vapors. It highlights his methodology of observing how surrounding materials shape a growing solid, a foundational principle of crystallography.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Steno, crystal growth, accretion, mineralogy, geological vapors, formation of stones</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicolaus Steno’s geological theories. The previous page established Steno's revolutionary claim that crystals originate from a liquid state. This page elaborates on the mechanism of that formation, contrasting it with the creation of glass and the behavior of salts.</meta>

...beyond any doubt; yet he does not believe that they solidify through cold, or are produced from the ashes that have been turned into glass by the power of fire. For he is of the opinion that such a production cannot occur merely through the force of fire, but that it would also arise without fire and through human skill, if only one were to perform a precise dissection <note>original: "Zergliederung." Steno, originally a famous anatomist, applied the methods of biological dissection to the study of rocks to understand their internal structure.</note> of the stones in whose cavities the finest crystals are found. It is certain, he says, that the crystal—just as it arose from a fluid—can also be dissolved back into a liquid body, if only someone knew how to imitate nature’s true <term>solvent: the "menstruum," or liquid medium in which a solid is formed or dissolved.</term>. 

One should also not say that bodies from which all <term>solvent</term> has been driven away by the force of fire can no longer be dissolved; the nature of those fused bodies that grow in the midst of a liquid or mineral fluid <note>original: "Saft," literally "sap." 17th-century scientists often used botanical terms like "sap" or "juice" to describe the mineral-rich waters they believed circulated through the Earth like blood.</note>, and whose parts are left behind between the particles of the solidified body, is quite different. For the liquid in which the crystal grows behaves toward the crystal just as common water behaves toward salts. It is a completely different matter with glass that has been prepared by the power of fire, for its moisture is driven away almost entirely.

<vocab>Crystal, Solvent, Fire, Glass, Dissection, Salts</vocab>

I do recall, however, that even with common glass, one can obtain a specific fluid <note>original: "Saft," literally "sap" or "juice." 17th-century scientists often used botanical terms like "sap" to describe the mineral-rich liquids they believed circulated through the Earth and influenced the growth of solids.</note> which makes it flexible for at least a short time, even if it does not fully dissolve it. Indeed, it is likely that since the glass body is prepared from various already solid substances—which are only dissolved by their own specific fluids—there is no single fluid that can act effectively upon bodies that are composed in such a complex way.

The account of gemstones artificially prepared using water and a <term>coagulating: the process of thickening or solidifying a liquid into a solid mass.</term> powder, which I mentioned in the aforementioned letter, contributes much to the clarification of this matter. I shall not repeat it here, as that treatise was published last year and is in everyone's hands. Furthermore, some light may be shed by an experiment I witnessed in Amsterdam at the home of Mr. Birri, a physician known through several chemical books. He showed me fine, quite transparent stones, cut like gemstones, which he assured me he had made from a certain fluid that he displayed. This fluid was far heavier,

And when the fire was increased, five parts of it remained perfectly fixed—that is to say, a half-ounce of that fixed liquor remained from an ounce and a half of quicksilver. Had it been further purified and then joined like a soul to its proportionate body, one could have formed the true stone <note>the true stone: A reference to the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> with it; but until now I have never been able to find it again..."

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's personal reflection on the nature of minerals, following a quoted anecdote from Francesco Lana de Terzi. The catchword from the previous page was "Ore, Gold Liquo...", leading into this page's discussion on the composition of metals and stones.</meta>

-> ✠ ✠ ✠ <-

Since I consider metals to be **composite bodies** <note>original Latin: "Decomposita," referring to substances made of multiple simpler components.</note>, which others call compounds, they may perhaps contain a **liquid essence** <term>liquid essence: original "Liquor," a fundamental alchemical fluid believed to reside within solid matter.</term> in their primordial basic parts; however, this essence does not appear during the formation of this or that metal, nor is it of any use in the artificial production of metals. More on this will follow below. As for the **solidification** <note>original: "Gerinnung," literally "clotting" or "coagulation."</note> of gemstones and crystals from water, it seems to me that the matter is not yet sufficiently settled. For although they seem to approach the nature of water because of their transparency and simpler composition, many doubts remain which give reason to believe that the truth may be otherwise. I admit that they are transparent, but they are also hard and brittle, which is a sign that there is less moisture in them than in metals, whose **fibrous toughness** <note>original: "spießigte Zähigkeit," describing a splintery or needle-like internal structure common in certain ores.</note> clearly reveals a liquid essence. **Bernard Palissy** <note>Bernard Palissy (c. 1510–1589) was a famous French potter and natural philosopher who studied the origins of springs and the formation of fossils.</note>, who derives them from water, believes they are composed of a double liquid essence; one of these, which he calls the **coagulating** one, is nothing other than a salt—as **Gorellus** <note>likely a reference to Giovanni Alfonso Borelli (1608–1679), an Italian physicist and mathematician.</note> explains—or another stone-making substance that is different from the nature of water. Thus, it is evident from his own principles that crystal is not a body consisting of uniform parts.

<summary>The author disputes the idea that gemstones and metals are formed from pure water, arguing instead that they are complex compounds containing "salts" and "liquid essences." He cites the theories of Bernard Palissy and Borelli to support the idea that even transparent crystals are composed of diverse substances.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, mineralogy, metal composition, crystallization, Bernard Palissy, Borelli, liquid essence, salts, transparency</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s investigation into the formation of minerals. Having previously discussed the composition of metals and the theories of Bernard Palissy, the author now pivots to the specific role of fluids and "juices" in the creation of common stones versus precious gems.</meta>

-> ❄ ❁ ❄ <-

That common stones are composed of very coarse parts is shown, among other things, by the observation of **Peireskius** <note>Nicolas-Claude Fabri de Peiresc (1580–1637) was a French astronomer and polymath known for his wide-ranging scientific correspondence.</note>, which **Gassendus** <note>Pierre Gassendi (1592–1655) was a French philosopher and scientist who wrote a celebrated biography of Peiresc.</note> recounts in his life story: how he pulled a thick slime out of the water which, after the air touched it, turned into stone. Should one then not also be able to assume in the case of gemstones and crystals that various juices <note>original: "Säfte," a term used by early naturalists to describe the mineral-rich fluids believed to circulate underground.</note> or earths, which are of a purer nature than the common ones, mix with one another? However, it is said that **Steno** <note>Nicolaus Steno (1638–1686) was a Danish pioneer in geology who first described how crystal faces grow from a fluid.</note> found traces of water at the very first beginning of crystals, and thus they must have arisen from it. But what if the water was only the **vehicle** <note>original Latin: "Vehiculum," meaning a carrier substance that transports other materials to a specific location without becoming part of them.</note> or that which guided the fundamental parts of the gemstones to their locations, but otherwise did not mix with them? 

Furthermore, I do not believe that a valid proof can be derived from saying: crystals and gemstones are dissolved by this or that **liquid** <term>liquid: original Latin "Liquor," here referring to a chemical solvent or alchemical fluid.</term>, and consequently they are also composed of these juices. Many liquids can indeed be prepared through art <note>In this context, "art" refers to the practice of chemistry or alchemy.</note> whose particles are so constituted that they penetrate into the tiny gaps of solid bodies, setting them apart and separating them, even though they contribute nothing to their composition. In England, by chance, a vegetable **liquid** <note>original Latin: "Liquor."</note> was discovered that [softened] the hardest mar- <note>The text cuts off here, likely intending to say "marble" (Marmor).</note>

<summary>The author challenges the idea that gemstones are made of water, suggesting instead that water acts merely as a carrier for purer "juices" and "earths." He argues that the ability of a solvent to dissolve a stone does not prove the stone was originally made of that liquid.</summary>

<keywords>mineralogy, Peiresc, Pierre Gassendi, Nicolaus Steno, crystal formation, solvents, crystallization, geological fluids</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the theory that gemstones are merely solidified liquids. ...[mar]ble, and having added colors, tinted it in its innermost parts. **Berigard** <note>Claude Guillermet de Bérigard (1578–1663) was a French philosopher and scientist.</note>, as **Pisanus** <note>The author refers to Bérigard by his Latinized name, Pisanus, after the University of Pisa where he taught.</note> recounts in his **Pisan Circle** <note>original: "Circulo Pisano," a work published in 1643 that blended Aristotelian and atomist philosophy.</note> on page 534, easily dissolved pearls and other things with the first phlegm <term>phlegm: In early chemistry and alchemy, "phlegm" was the watery, tasteless distillate that came over first during the distillation process.</term> that passes over from the sharpest vinegar. However, with the last distillate, which is driven by the strongest fire and is far sharper and more colored, he could accomplish nothing. Nevertheless, neither the pearls nor the marble were created from this liquid. Indeed, even supposing we had the liquid from which the crystal-making substance had either grown together or precipitated; I still do not believe, as the clever **Steno** <note>Nicolaus Steno (1638–1686), the Danish pioneer of geology and anatomy.</note> maintains, that they could thereby be transformed back into a fluid state. Finally, it might seem probable to some that gemstones—if not all, then at least some—could be produced from various bodies through intense fire, just as glass is made. At the very least, those devoted to the art of gold-making <note>original: "Goldmacherkunst," a common term for alchemy.</note> teach, if I remember correctly, how they can be made in fire; indeed, those false gemstones which **Antonius Nerus** <note>Antonio Neri (1576–1614) was an Italian priest whose book "The Art of Glass" revealed the secrets of making colored glass and imitation gems.</note> teaches how to make, serve as a sort of model for this.

For some contemplate a certain universal salt, from which they expect—heaven knows what miracles—not only in all of nature but also in the mineral kingdom. This opinion has arisen since the time of **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note>, and has been passed down to our times by some of his followers, as well as the **Rosicrucians** <note>A secret philosophical society that emerged in the early 17th century, claiming to possess esoteric wisdom regarding nature and the spiritual realm.</note>. They all call this salt a heavenly, airy, ethereal salt, which they extract either from dew or from the air, from nitre <term>nitre: Also known as saltpeter; in alchemy, it was often considered a "fiery" earth salt essential to life and minerals.</term>, or even from the excrement of animals. Whether such a salt exists, I will not decide. The old school of chemists <note>original: "Chymisten"</note> at least knew nothing of it. I do not wish to deny that one can extract a salt from the air, but I do not believe it is a universal salt. Particles of all kinds of salt float about in the air; these are made subtle by subterranean fire or the heat of the sun and rise into the atmosphere according to the nature of the soil found beneath them. Thus, they are sometimes nitrous <term>nitrous: Containing or relating to nitre.</term>, sometimes vitriolic <term>vitriolic: Derived from vitriol, an early term for various sulfate minerals such as iron or copper sulfate.</term>, and sometimes of another nature,

On this occasion, I am reminded of what I saw in Amsterdam at the home of the industrious Mr. Theodor Kerkring <note>Theodor Kerckring (1638–1693) was a Dutch physician and anatomist famous for his chemical research and his collection of anatomical specimens.</note>. He showed me a considerable supply of true and genuine, yet impure, vitriol <term>vitriol: A historical term for sulfate minerals (like copper or iron sulfate); in this context, it refers to metallic salts crystallized from the environment.</term>, which he had extracted from the Amsterdam air—which is saturated with many salty vapors and moisture rising from the marshy ground—by means of a machine. For the great instruments of architectonic Nature are fire and salts; yet these vary according to the diversity of bodies and their mixtures, and Nature employs them in dissolution and composition according to the properties of the subjects. Nevertheless, such things do not constitute a part of their essential being.

By means of salts, Nature produces wonderful changes in all bodies, including metallic ones, which the subterranean furnaces scatter throughout the entire earth, through all veins and ore mines. Therefore, one encounters these distinct particles of salts and sulfur <note>original: "Schwefel." In early chemistry, sulfur was often viewed as the "principle of combustibility" rather than just the yellow mineral we know today.</note> in the mines; however, one cannot claim without audacity that they are constituent parts of the metals themselves. ...[produced] all the ingredients: true vitriol <term>vitriol: Historically, a term for various sulfate minerals; here it likely refers to a metallic salt extracted from the air or soil.</term>, true sulfur, common salt, and vinegar of saltpetre <note>original: "Salpeteressig." For when the sounding lead <note>original: "Bleywurf." ->§. 4.<-

Furthermore, there are others who abandon this universal salt <term>universal salt: In alchemy, a hypothetical "philosopher's salt" or "alkahest" believed to be a universal solvent or the root of all minerals.</term> because they hunt for it everywhere and find it nowhere; instead, they settle upon common and well-known salts. They observe the powers of nitre <term>nitre: Potassium nitrate, also known as saltpeter.</term>, vitriol <term>vitriol: Sulfate minerals; "green vitriol" is iron sulfate, "blue vitriol" is copper sulfate.</term>, and common salt in the separation of metals, and therefore suspect special secrets lie within them. All sorts of little stories, which they have gathered from oral accounts or books, give them courage. Such stories include silver being drawn from lead with the help of the spirit of salt <term>spirit of salt: An archaic name for hydrochloric acid.</term>, found in Johann Friedrich Helvetius’s *The Golden Calf* <note>original: "Joh. Friedr. Helvetius in seinem goldenen Kalbe." A famous alchemical treatise published in 1667 describing a purported transmutation of lead into gold.</note>; and a good portion of gold being brought out of good Hungarian vitriol, mentioned by Becher in his *Underground Physics* <note>original: "Becher in seiner unterirdischen Physik." Refers to Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682) and his seminal work *Physica Subterranea*, which laid the groundwork for the phlogiston theory.</note>. However, such things arose much more from a separation <term>separation: original "Scheidung," the process of refining or "parting" precious metals from base metals in an alloy.</term> of the gold and silver already hidden within them, rather than being special "introductions" <note>The author is arguing that the gold and silver were already present as impurities in the lead or vitriol, and the chemical process merely revealed them rather than "creating" or "introducing" them through alchemy.</note> of the same; for I will [not give...] these people who tell it...

...not make faith doubtful. Yet I confess that I do not believe it merely by chance—what Salomon von Blauenstein <note>Salomon von Blauenstein was a pseudonym, likely for the alchemist and physician Constantin Leopold Heymann, who famously debated with Father Athanasius Kircher regarding the possibility of transmutation.</note> (as he calls himself by this fictional name, and who presents himself as a true student of the gold-making art) writes in his *Interpellation to the Philosophers on Behalf of the Philosopher’s Stone, Against Kircher’s Underground World* <note>original: "wider Kirchers unterirdische Welt," referring to Athanasius Kircher’s 1665 work *Mundus Subterraneus*, which expressed deep skepticism about alchemical gold-making.</note>, Chapter 2: "What need is there for much?" he says. "I could even convince Father Kircher, if he were with me for only three hours, and place faith into the hands of the unbeliever, showing how from pure, simple mercury, through the addition of a prepared simple salt, a bright and pure gold is made." 

What he understands by his "simple prepared salt," I do not know; if it is common salt, then I confess nothing more wonderful could be devised that would be so contrary to the principles of the chemists. Many have worked especially with vitriol <term>vitriol: Sulfate minerals, such as iron or copper sulfate, which were foundational in early chemistry for producing acids.</term> and saltpeter <term>saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, used both in gunpowder and as a powerful reagent in the laboratory.</term>, even to the point of madness. Some have written entire books on the subject and wished to show the way to others, though they themselves were blind; for they did not understand the pronouncements of the chemists, which deceived them through the variety of words used. Nor could they explain them by comparing the authors, but instead interpreted them according to their own opinions—whereas for those authors, vitriol and nitre <term>nitre: Another name for saltpeter or mineral salts; a term often used ambiguously in alchemical texts.</term> mean something entirely different. How many processes from vitriol are not by the...

He cannot name it himself, and among all those that are known, none is capable of such a thing.

->§. 4.<-

Furthermore, there are others who abandon this universal salt <note>A hypothetical "prima materia" or all-purpose reagent sought by alchemists.</note> because they hunt for it everywhere and find it nowhere; instead, they settle upon common and well-known salts. Namely, they observe the powers of niter <term>niter: Also known as saltpeter; potassium nitrate, used as a powerful solvent and a key ingredient in gunpowder.</term>, vitriol <term>vitriol: A term for sulfate minerals, which were used to produce strong mineral acids like sulfuric acid.</term>, and common salt in the separation <note>The process of "parting," or separating gold from silver and other metals.</note> of metals, and thus suspect special secrets lie within them. All sorts of little stories, which they gather from tales or books, give them courage. Examples include silver being drawn from lead with the help of spirit of salt <term>spirit of salt: An early name for hydrochloric acid, produced by distilling salt.</term>, found in Johann Friedrich Helvetius in his *The Golden Calf* <note>original: "goldenen Kalbe," referring to the 1667 work *Vitulus Aureus*, in which Helvetius claimed to have witnessed the transmutation of lead into gold.</note>; or a good portion of gold being brought out of good Hungarian vitriol, as found in Becher in his *Underground Physics* <note>original: "unterirdische Physik," referring to Johann Joachim Becher's *Physica Subterranea* (1669), a foundational text for early chemistry.</note>. However, such results have arisen much more from a separation of the gold and silver already hidden within those materials than from any special introduction of the same; for I [do not wish to] these people, who tell of it,

faith <note>catchword: "Glauben" (faith/belief), providing a bridge to the next page's discussion on the credibility of alchemists.</note>

<summary>The author dismisses the theory of a "universal salt" as mere conjecture and critiques alchemists who believe common reagents like vitriol or niter can create gold. He argues that these alchemists are simply extracting trace amounts of precious metals already present in their source materials rather than achieving true transmutation.</summary>

...not make faith doubtful. However, I confess that I do not believe on a whim what Salomon von Blauenstein <note>Salomon von Blauenstein: A pseudonym for an author who wrote in defense of alchemy against contemporary skeptics.</note>—as he calls himself by that fabricated name, and who presents himself as a true student of the **art of gold-making** <term>gold-making: Formally known as chrysopoeia, the alchemical pursuit of creating gold from base metals.</term>—writes in his *Interpellation to the Philosophers for the Philosopher’s Stone* <note>original: "Interpellation an die Philosophen für den Stein der Weisen"</note>, in opposition to Kircher’s *Subterranean World* <note>Father Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680) was a famous Jesuit scholar who argued in his book *Mundus Subterraneus* that alchemical transmutation was impossible.</note>, Chapter 2: "What more is needed?" he says, "I could even convince Father Kircher, if he were with me for only three hours, and place faith into the hand of the unbeliever, showing how pure and plain **gold** is made from nothing but mere **mercury** through the addition of a prepared simple salt." What he means by his "simple prepared salt," I do not know; if it is common salt, I admit that nothing more wonderful could be imagined, for it would be utterly contrary to the principles of the chemists. 

In particular, many have labored unto madness with **vitriol** <term>vitriol: Historically, a sulfate mineral (like copper or iron sulfate) used to create powerful acids.</term> and **saltpeter**. Some have written entire books about them, wishing to show the way to others while they themselves were blind; for they did not understand the pronouncements of the chemists—who deceived them through a diversity of words—nor could they explain them by comparing the authors to see where their opinions aligned, in whose works "vitriol" and "**niter**" <term>niter: Also known as saltpeter or potassium nitrate; a key ingredient in gunpowder and a powerful reagent in early chemistry.</term> mean something entirely different <note>Alchemists often used "Decknamen" (cover names), where common words like "vitriol" referred to secret substances rather than the common minerals.</note>. ...substance, which is boiled out little by little, so that the weathering of the air and the power of water can no longer harm it. However, all of this is asserted as a mere conjecture without any appearance of truth. What kind of salty substance would this be? He cannot name it himself, and among all those that are known, none is capable of such a thing.

->Section 4<-

There are furthermore others who abandon this universal salt <term>universal salt: In alchemical theory, a hypothetical "prime salt" or "sal circulatum" believed to be the fundamental building block of all matter.</term> because they hunt for it everywhere and find it nowhere; instead, they settle on common and well-known salts. Namely, they see the powers of saltpeter <term>saltpeter: Also known as niter; a mineral salt used in medicine, chemistry, and as a key ingredient in gunpowder.</term>, vitriol <term>vitriol: A historical term for sulfate minerals, such as iron or copper sulfate, often used to create powerful acids.</term>, and common salt in the separation of metals, and they suspect special secrets lie hidden within them. All sorts of little stories, which they have gathered from oral accounts or books, give them courage. 

Such accounts include silver being drawn out of lead with the help of spirit of salt <term>spirit of salt: An archaic name for hydrochloric acid, traditionally produced by distilling common salt with vitriol.</term>, as recorded by Johann Friedrich Helvetius <note>Johann Friedrich Helvetius (1630–1709) was a prominent physician who famously claimed to have received a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone from a stranger and performed a successful transmutation.</note> in his *The Golden Calf* <note>original: "goldenen Kalbe"</note>; or a good portion of gold being brought out of good Hungarian vitriol, as mentioned by Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682) was a German physician and alchemist whose work *Physica Subterranea* (Subterranean Physics) laid the groundwork for the later phlogiston theory.</note> in his *Subterranean Physics* <note>original: "unterirdische Physik"</note>. However, such things have arisen more from a separation <term>separation: In this context, "parting," or the process of refining and isolating precious metals that were already present as impurities in the base material.</term> of the gold and silver hidden within them than from any special introduction of those metals; for I wish to [accuse/question] these people who recount such things...

Many have worked especially with vitriol <term>vitriol: A historical term for sulfate minerals (like iron or copper sulfate), which were essential for producing strong acids.</term> and saltpeter <term>saltpeter: Also known as niter (potassium nitrate), a primary ingredient in gunpowder and various chemical "spirits."</term> to the point of madness. Some have written entire books about them and wished to show the way to others, though they themselves were blind; for they did not understand the pronouncements of the chemists, who deceived them through the diversity of words. Nor could they explain the authors' meaning through a comparison of different texts, but were instead led by their own opinions, in which vitriol and niter mean something entirely different <note>In alchemical "Deckname" (cover names), common terms like "vitriol" often referred to secret substances rather than the common minerals found in a pharmacy.</note>. ...carried around among the fraudsters? One finds a vitriol process <term>vitriol process: A specific laboratory procedure using sulfate minerals to attempt a chemical transformation, often aiming for the "philosopher's stone."</term> by a certain Jodocus von R . . . . , which is commonly found alongside the works of Basilius Valentinus <note>Basilius Valentinus was a legendary 15th-century alchemist, though the works attributed to him were likely written by others in the early 1600s.</note>. This process is said to provide the highest medicine for both humans and metals; it is written with great diligence and is quite laborious, yet a good friend who worked through it found it to have no effect and discovered it to be a lie. Furthermore, there is plenty of this sort of thing to go around. What a noise Glauber <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670) was a pioneering German-Dutch chemist and alchemist known for his discovery of sodium sulfate, still called "Glauber's salt."</note> made with his niter <term>niter: Also known as saltpeter (potassium nitrate), a key ingredient in gunpowder and strong acids.</term>, promising great wonders in so many books with much shouting? Quite a bit in them is unique and clever. Most of it, however, flowed more from his own imagination than from the heat of the furnaces. Moreover, we see no mountains of gold <note>original: "goldenen Berge." An idiom meaning "unfulfilled promises of great wealth."</note>. Nevertheless, Du Hamel <note>Jean-Baptiste Du Hamel (1624–1706), a prominent French scientist and the first secretary of the Academy of Sciences.</note> relied on him in many instances in his book on fossils <note>In the 17th century, the term "fossil" (from the Latin *fodere*, "to dig") referred to anything dug out of the earth, including minerals and ores, rather than just prehistoric remains.</note>, when he really should have tested him first. I do not wish to deny that through the help of these salts and niter, much that is wonderful can happen in nature; but whether they can produce a metal, I doubt, for they belong to an entirely different genus than the metals and are not made to generate that viscous metallic slime <note>Alchemists believed metals grew in the earth from a "metallic seed" or "slime" (*mucus*), which they sought to replicate in the laboratory.</note>. In the meantime, when they appear in the form of a sharp spirit <term>sharp spirit: A concentrated acid produced through distillation, such as nitric acid, which was known for its ability to dissolve metals.</term>, they exercise a tyrannical dominion over the metals; but if they are [treated] by a gentle fire

...shifted into a subtler and more pleasant nature, they even seem to draw a tincture <term>tincture: In alchemy, a medicinal or transformative extract, often thought to contain the concentrated essence of a substance.</term> out of the gold and carry it over the helm <note>helm: The top part or "head" of a still or alembic, where vapors collect and condense during distillation.</note>. Many make a great deal out of the spirit of the subtle niter-salt <term>niter-salt: Saltpeter (potassium nitrate), which many alchemists believed contained a "universal spirit" from the air.</term> from May dew <note>May dew: Dew collected in the early morning in May was highly prized by alchemists, as they believed it was impregnated with the "vital spirit" of the sun and the heavens.</note>. Nollius <note>Heinrich Nolle (Nollius) was a 17th-century German physician and follower of the alchemist Paracelsus.</note> writes: through the spirit <note>original: "Spiritum"</note> and the oil made from May dew, the sick could be rescued from death, and a tincture of gold could be made with it. Borellus <note>Pierre Borel (c. 1620–1671) was a French chemist, physician, and royal counselor known for his wide-ranging scientific observations.</note> in *Century 1, Observation 6*, who has gone even further, prides himself immensely on this secret. "After many labors and night-watches," he says, "which I spent in investigating the secrets of nature, I have finally found the secret of dissolving gold; that is, a gentle solvent <term>solvent: (Menstruum) A liquid used to dissolve a solid body.</term> which dissolves gold quite amicably <note>original: "freundschaftlich." The author uses this term to describe a gentle chemical reaction that occurs without violent bubbling, heat, or toxic fumes.</note> within a few hours without smoke, even without fire, whereby it assumes the nature of an oil and a salt. From this, 3, 4, to 6 grains <note>grain: A very small unit of weight, roughly 65 milligrams, used for measuring medicine.</note>, according to age, will cure malignant rashes <note>original: "Friesel." Likely referring to miliary fever, characterized by a rash of small red bumps.</note> and stubborn epidemic fevers in about 3 hours through profuse sweating. One should know, however, that it is made from the spirit of the dew according to the teachings of Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a world-famous Polish alchemist who taught that a "life-giving" saltpeter existed in the air and was captured by rain and dew.</note>."

<meta>This page continues the author's skeptical analysis of alchemical claims regarding dew and niter, specifically responding to the idea that these substances can "fix" or increase the weight of materials, before transitioning into a discussion of spontaneous generation.</meta>

-> ❦ ❦ ❦ <-

...dried in the sun and buried in the earth, it becomes heavier. However plausible all this is presented, it does not yet convince me that I should attribute to niter <term>niter: Also known as saltpeter (potassium nitrate); early chemists believed it was a "universal salt" that carried the life force of the atmosphere.</term>, May dew, or the spirit of honey a power that could fundamentally dissolve metals.

These liquids—or rather, salts mixed with liquid—do nothing else than what common corrosive waters <note>The author likely refers to "aqua fortis" (nitric acid) or other mineral acids used to dissolve metals.</note> do. Because they are more diluted and refined, they tear loose even the finest particles, which one can afterwards easily drive over the helm <term>helm: The "cap" or top part of a distillation apparatus (alembic) where vapors collect before condensing.</term> using fire, though they have not yet been transformed out of their original nature. This is my opinion on niter, or the nitrous salt found within dew, in relation to metalwork; otherwise, its great power in the generation of plants and animals is well known to me.

In place of a digression, I will provide a specific example of this. I would never have dreamed that one could create eels through art <note>In this context, "art" refers to "alchemy" or human-directed technology, as opposed to "nature."</note>, nor have I read of such a thing in any natural philosopher; nevertheless, on my recent journey to Holland, I learned that the farmers achieve this through the use of dew. At first, I did not want to believe the story, but afterwards I came across **Abraham Mylius**'s work *On the Origin of Animals and the Migration of Peoples* <note>original: "De origine animalium et migratione populorum" (1594) by Abraham Mylius, which discussed linguistics and the origin of species.</note>, in which I read of this very feat on page 10; I wish to record his words here.

This is made all the more probable because one finds neither seed nor reproductive organs in eels, which is why much dispute has arisen among natural philosophers regarding their origin <note>Since antiquity, the reproduction of eels was a mystery; Aristotle famously believed they emerged spontaneously from mud ("the guts of the earth").</note>. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the generative power of salts and dew, specifically referencing Athanasius Kircher’s promised works on the subject, before transitioning into a new section (§ 5) regarding the nature of metals and the properties of mercury (quicksilver).</meta>

[writ-]ten, and Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who wrote extensively on magnetism, light, and the "Great Art of Knowledge."</note> has promised a whole book on the subject. The power of this salt is thus great enough for the production of plants and animals; however, while metals can indeed be attacked and tormented <note>In early modern laboratory practice, "tormenting" a substance referred to subjecting it to extreme heat, acids, or distillation to force it to reveal its hidden properties.</note> by it, they can in no way be transformed, increased, or made fruitful like plants.

---

->§ 5.<-

From the category of salts, we proceed directly to the metals. Among these, quicksilver <note>Mercury.</note> deserves to be mentioned first of all—this wonderful substance that dons and disguises itself in a thousand-fold forms, no matter how artfully one may attempt to manipulate it. Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), the Roman author of *Natural History*.</note> calls it the "tinder of the eternal liquor" <note>original: "Zunder des ewigen Liquors." This refers to mercury’s unique state as a "liquid metal" that seems to contain a perpetual, flowing essence.</note>; for it is soft and liquid. And since it embraces gold with a particular love and unites itself most intimately with it <note>A reference to amalgamation, where mercury dissolves gold.</note>—appearing even more than other metals to be the radical moisture <term>radical moisture</term> <note>In alchemy, *humidum radicale* was believed to be the fundamental life-fluid or the primary moist essence that allowed metals to grow and stay flexible.</note> and the origin of metal—many have believed they have found in it, so to speak, the primal essence of the metals. Indeed, even now there are many who have not become any wiser through the failures of others who have suffered "shipwreck" <note>An alchemical metaphor for wasting one's fortune and life on failed experiments to create gold.</note> in this pursuit. Added to this is its venerable name, Mercury, which is encountered so frequently in chymical <note>Early modern science used "chymistry" to encompass both what we now call chemistry and alchemy.</note> writings, alongside many descriptions and requirements, such that even the most keen-witted are deceived.

Some desire a virgin Mercury <note>Mercury found in its native, liquid state in nature, rather than smelted from ore.</note>, which nature brings forth and which has not yet been in the fire; others take Mercury that has been drawn out of other metals through art <note>In this context, "art" refers to the alchemical or chemical processes used to extract or "revive" mercury from other metallic substances.</note>. Whether this can happen, many have until now doubted, but they have been taught the truth of this feat <note>original: "Kunststück"</note> by your letter*, my Lord! For my part, I believe that there is no difference between such [mercury] and common quicksilver, let others say what they will. For since its division into the smallest particles is wonderful and almost imperceptible, which happens in the mines through the subterranean fire <note>Early modern scientists believed that heat within the Earth's crust was responsible for the formation and movement of minerals.</note>; so it creeps very easily into the metals and mixes with them, but not fundamentally <note>original: "von Grund aus." The author is arguing that mercury forms a physical mixture or alloy (amalgam) rather than a permanent new chemical identity.</note>. Some particles of it go into the air during the first melting of the metal, but those which cling more stubbornly to them are finally separated from them again with the help of salts. This happens more easily with ores <note>original: "Minern"</note>, into which it finds a freer entrance, and thus is also chased away from them more quickly. But if the metallic particles are more closely combined with it during melting, it requires more effort. From antimony <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Spiesglas" (stibnite). A metalloid frequently used in alchemical purification processes.</note> and from lead it cannot be brought out as quickly as from their

<meta>This page continues from page 35, transitioning from a discussion of mercury found naturally in ores and organic matter to the more theoretical "mercury of the philosophers." The reasons cited above are so plausible that one easily falls into this opinion. However, all experiments conducted so far have brought us to nothing certain, and the honest chemists <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymisten"; in the 17th and 18th centuries, the terms "chemist" and "alchemist" were often used interchangeably to describe those who studied the composition of matter.</note> mentioned all unanimously confess: that they have nothing to do with common mercury <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurius"; refers here to ordinary quicksilver as opposed to the "Mercury of the Wise," a purified philosophical principle.</note>. They do not wish to be understood as referring to a special quicksilver—which Lullius <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many influential alchemical treatises were later attributed.</note> in a certain place calls a "wind-egg" <note>original: "ovum subventaneum"; a term for an unfertilized or "empty" egg, used here metaphorically to describe a substance that lacks the vital "seed" or potential for alchemical growth.</note>—but rather another certain metallic substance, which is immature but not impure in its root. Without this, they say, quicksilver has no use in the transmutation of metals. I could confirm this with many hundreds of their sayings, were it not already well known. Yet I can cite some who boast of having produced a noble metal from quicksilver via the so-called "particular way" <term>particular way</term> <note>original: "Partikularweg"; a process in alchemy intended to produce a specific, limited result (like transforming a small amount of metal) rather than the "Universal" medicine or Philosopher's Stone that could transform all matter.</note>, of which I will speak below.

How many works have been undertaken with antimony <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonio"; a brittle, silvery-white metalloid. In alchemy, it was highly valued for its ability to "purify" gold, leading many to believe it held the secret to the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in order to bring forth the Great Work of Nature? However, the true mercury required for the composition of noble metals is not found in it either, as is evident from its brittle structure. Some pure sulfur <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "Schwefel"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter, whereas mercury represents the fluid, passive, and feminine principle.</note> may be within it, through which a partial transformation might be granted. -> Ӂ ❁ Ӂ <-

This is also suggested by **Basilius Valentinus** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, now believed to be a pseudonym for Johann Thölde, who published "The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony" in 1604.</note> in the **Triumphal Chariot of Antimony** <note>original: "Triumph-wagen des Antimonii"; one of the most famous alchemical texts, focusing on the chemical and medicinal properties of antimony.</note> and his commentator **Theodor Kerkring** <note>Theodor Kerckring (1638–1693), a Dutch physician and anatomist known for his commentary on Valentinus.</note>. For by their own admission, it contributes nothing to the Great Work <term>Great Work</term> <note>original: "großen Werke" or *Magnum Opus*; the ultimate alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Yet the most useful medicines lie hidden within it, especially for diseases stemming from infected blood, which it is capable of suppressing in a wondrous way. **Basilius** raises its praise to the heavens, and **Kerkring** confirms it with his experiments. There is also no shortage of those who believe they can produce a more perfect metal through the marriage of two metals—one perfect and one imperfect—simply through long-lasting boiling. By doing so, they believe they are imitating the subterranean operations of nature, as they see that in nature, lead ore <note>original: "Bleyminera"</note> is transformed into silver over time.

This was the opinion of the sharp-minded naturalist **Bacon of Verulam** <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), the influential English philosopher and father of the scientific method.</note>, who in his **Natural History, Century 4** <note>original: "Histor. Nat. Cent. 4"; referring to Bacon's *Sylva Sylvarum*, a collection of observations and experiments organized into "centuries" or groups of one hundred.</note> prescribes the manner in which it seemed possible to him to transform a base metal into a nobler one. After citing the necessary requirements, he finally commands in **No. 327** to approach the matter thus: 
“Construct a narrow furnace and provide moderate heat, so that the metal remains in a constant state of flow, but no stronger, which is the primary task. Take silver, as it is that which comes closest to gold; upon it, add the tenth [part]...”

Nothing certain is known of its origin. Some hold it to be a product of the sea; others believe it comes forth from the earth, which opinion I consider more probable. The small animals and insects found within it also testify to this, as our colleague **Dr. Major** <note>Johann Daniel Major (1634–1693), a German physician and polymath interested in natural history and fossils.</note> will eventually show in detail in his book *On the Maritime Origins of Amber* <note>original: "de succini natalibus maritimis." Published in 1667, Major used this work to argue that amber was a mineral resin from the earth, despite its frequent discovery on shores.</note>. It is, however, frequently produced in those places where fir trees and turpentine trees grow, which provides proof that it comes from a similar viscous substance <note>original: "viscosischen Substanz." ### Section 7.

Now let us enter the school of the **chemists** <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymisten." At the time of this writing, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of matter and its transformations.</note> and hear their pronouncements. These people praise their **Mercury** <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurius." In alchemy, this is not just the liquid metal (quicksilver), but a "principle" representing fluidity, volatility, and the spirit of a substance.</note> and their **Sulfur** <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Schwefel." This represents the principle of "combustibility" or the soul; it was believed to be the oily, fatty substance that allows things to burn.</note> to us. If one investigates what they wish to be understood by these names (for they are not to be taken in the common sense), they will recite a hundred kinds of words and dark, enigmatic descriptions to us, from which one can catch and pick out a meaning only with great effort.

However, if one has made it that far, one will find a certain agreement among them, except that the newest sect of chemists, following the time of **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who revolutionized medicine by arguing that diseases were caused by external agents and could be treated with minerals.</note>, have added a third **principle** <term>principle</term> <note>original: "Principium." A fundamental building block of nature.</note> to Mercury and Sulfur—namely, **Salt** <term>Salt</term> <note>original: "Salz." Paracelsus added Salt to represent the "body" or the solid, earthy residue left after combustion.</note>. I do not wish to investigate extensively here whether there are truly so many in number, and whether they are to be found in all bodies, or if they are only distinct from the common ones. For **Mr. Boyle** <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), an Anglo-Irish scientist often called the "Father of Modern Chemistry."</note> and **Mr. du Hamel** <note>Jean-Baptiste du Hamel (1624–1706), a French scientist and philosopher. The work cited is *De consensu veteris et novae philosophiae* (On the Agreement of Old and New Philosophy).</note> have taught and sufficiently elaborated on this—the former in his *The Sceptical Chymist* <note>original: "Chymista Sceptico." This 1661 book challenged the traditional alchemical view of the four elements and the three principles.</note>, and the latter in his book *On the Agreement of Old and New Philosophy*, Book 2, Chapter 4. The ancients before Paracelsus, however, only assumed these two principles, namely Mercury and Sulfur—not common ones, but metallic ones—

"In the mines of the **Count of Hohenstein** in the **Harz** <note>The Harz Mountains in northern Germany, one of the most important silver-mining regions in Europe during the Early Modern period.</note>, primarily on the **Endersberg**, at the most famous mine called the **Samson** <note>The Samson Mine (Grube Samson) in Sankt Andreasberg, renowned for its rich silver deposits and historical depth.</note>, this memorable and unheard-of thing occurred: that a white, flowing, **native silver** <term>native silver</term> <note>Silver found in its metallic state in nature, rather than as a chemical compound within an ore.</note> was encountered there, like quicksilver, which flowed from the vein and outside, which men gathered up with their hands; and as soon as it was placed in the fire, it immediately became refined—of which I have been informed by credible people. As some say, the **ore** <note>original: "Erzt."</note> was just like a buttermilk, but as soon as it was held in the air for a while, or even kept in vessels in which one intended to keep it soft, it became hard, like sand or grit, and the white color also changed to brown or rusty." 

What the author relates here of this wonderful matter almost agrees with that which

**Mathesius** <note>Johann Mathesius (1504–1565), a German minister and mineralogist famous for his "mining sermons" (Sarepta), which combined theology with detailed observations of 16th-century mining technology.</note> speaks of the **Gur** <term>Gur</term> <note>original: "Gur"; in early modern mining lore, "Guhr" was a semi-liquid, milky, or pasty mineral substance found in rock fissures, believed by miners to be the "mother" or the "unripe" embryonic state of metals.</note>, which has not yet attained the nature of pure silver. This same **Albinus** cites on page 127 of his aforementioned book several more remarkable things, which we shall pass over so as not to fill the space with more testimonies. Perhaps that which **Nirenberg** <note>Juan Eusebio Nieremberg (1595–1658), a Spanish Jesuit whose *Historia naturae* (1635) was a major natural history encyclopedia documenting the New World.</note> relates in his *Natural History*, Book 16, Chapter 19, concerning a particular metal used for the dissolution of silver, is worthy of some attention.

„Mercury," he says, „was known only to the barbarians, but was not useful; for instead of it, for the processing of silver, another metal was used, which was found on another low hill lying next to the **Potosi** <note>The Cerro Rico de Potosí in modern-day Bolivia, which was the richest silver-mining district in the world during the 16th and 17th centuries.</note> mine shaft. The Indians call it **huayna Potochy**, the 'Young Potochy.' This lesser metal, almost like lead, was added to the silver instead of mercury. They call it **Zuruchec** <note>Likely a phonetic rendering of a Quechua term related to "soroche," a name for argentiferous lead ores or galena used as a flux in the smelting process to help the silver flow.</note>, which means 'making it fall apart' <note>original: "auseinander fallend machend"; referring to the way the flux helps the metal separate from the waste rock.</note>, because through it the silver became soft so that it did not burn.” 

These are the traces I have encountered in the writings of the chemists concerning the **First Principle** <term>First Principle</term> <note>In the alchemical tradition, the "First Principle" refers to the fundamental substance—often called "Philosophical Mercury"—that serves as the base of all metals.</note> of metals, **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"; here the author refers to the alchemical principle of Mercury, rather than just the common liquid metal.</note>, which they hide so thoroughly that one cannot penetrate their secrets; for they conceal nothing more carefully than that upon which the entire business of **Alchemy** depends.

※ ◌ ※

The second **Principle** <term>Principium</term> <note>From the Latin *principium*, meaning a fundamental source or beginning. In the alchemical theory of the *Tria Prima* (Three Primes) popularized by Paracelsus, all matter is composed of Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury.</note> is **Sulphur**, which is spiritual and penetrating; it also coagulates metallic matter, and its sources are not kept secret by the masters of the art. They seek to extract it from gold, though only after the gold has first been dissolved by that first principle <note>Refers to Mercury, which was discussed previously as the "first principle" and the primary solvent or "mother" of metals.</note>; for they reject the sulphurs of other metals as impure. 

Some trace its primary origin to the sun and the stars; however, this is entirely uncertain. Others attribute it to the **subterranean fire**, which is not quite so absurd. For since the earth contains within itself all the primary natures necessary for the generation of mixed things, one must conclude that within it resides a great power of fire, without which nothing can be generated or mixed. 

This fire must be related to various kinds of oily natures with which it is first mixed; hence, at times purer, and at other times more impure or not-yet-refined oily exhalations are expelled. When these are mixed with water or earth, they seem to bring forth all manner of species. These include not only the types of sulphurs, resins, and the like known to us, but also many more unknown to us, to which fire is particularly related, contributing of its own accord to the mixture so that they cannot be separated. For the combustion of oily things seems to be nothing other than a separation—

Since the **Sulphur of the Chemists** is in fact an oily but incombustible matter, and yet a "corporeal fire," as they say, or a **fire of nature**: it is probable that it originates from the first mixture. Du Hamel <note>Jean-Baptiste Du Hamel (1624–1706), a French scientist and philosopher.</note> in *On Fossils* <note>original: *De fossilibus*, Book 2, Chapter 9, Page 246.</note> wishes to derive this Sulphur neither from the stars nor from the **central fire** <note>The alchemical belief in a literal fire at the center of the Earth that generates minerals.</note>, but considers it to be identical to common sulphur, except that through a long process of change it has become **fixed** <term>fix</term> <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating or changing.</note>, pure, and indestructible. Whatever mercury remains beyond that weight is <term>fixed</term> <note>To "fix" a volatile substance like mercury was to make it solid and resistant to heat, a key step in creating precious metals.</note> into silver. so from the common mixture of the purple-colored and white particles the yellow color arises, just as from the composition of copper and zinc mixed in the right proportion a substance arises that can be compared with the best gold in terms of its color and external appearance. Thus, if the **sulfur** <note>In this context, 'sulfur' refers to the alchemical principle of color and inflammability, not just the chemical element S.</note> is gone or drawn out, the gold becomes white, which is commonly called <term>fixed moon</term> <note>original: *Lunam fixam*; in alchemy, 'Luna' (the moon) refers to silver. Furthermore, it proves that the sulfur—or the color of gold—is of a different nature than the gold itself, because the color can be enhanced and increased through **cementations** <note>original: *Cemente*; a process of refining or altering metals by heating them in contact with powdered chemical reagents.</note> of sulfurous, though impure, things. Gold can be mixed with certain materials and roasted by fire so that, as it turns into a **regulus** <term>metallic mass</term> <note>original: *Regulum*; the heavy metallic portion of an ore that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting.</note>, it is at first red, then lemon-colored, and finally so weak in color that it is hardly considered gold. From which it is clear that the best parts of the sulfur are **precipitated** in the first smelting, just as the best parts in a first distillation tend to fall to the bottom, so that they can be more closely united and also set apart from one another. Experienced men have shared that Rhenish gold <note>Gold harvested from the sands of the Rhine river, typically known for having a higher silver content and thus a paler yellow color.</note>, which is paler in color and therefore less valuable, can be so enhanced through **cementation** <term>cementing</term> <note>original: *Cemente*; a metallurgical process where a metal is heated below its melting point while surrounded by powdered chemical reagents to refine it or change its color.</note> that it becomes equal to Hungarian gold <note>Hungarian gold was historically famous for its high purity and deep, rich color.</note>. A certain Bohemian nobleman confirmed this to me when I was in England, telling me of a Venetian who had amassed such wealth through this art that the Council <note>Likely referring to the Council of Ten, the governing body of the Republic of Venice.</note> ordered an investigation into him; it then happened that his profit ceased once the cause of his wealth was discovered. That this already happened in France in former times was made probable to me by **Thomas Freigius** <note>Thomas Freig (1543–1583), a German humanist scholar and polymath.</note> in his *Physics* <note>original: *Physicorum*; specifically Book 26, p. 708.</note>, who believes that the Rhenish gold guilders were mixed there with nobler gold. For the French have forbidden the export of Rhenish gold. Furthermore, many thousands of guilders were sent to France by merchants, yet they were never seen there again; presumably, they were melted down and recast. The Rhenish gold was either mixed with nobler gold or brought to a higher color by other means. At the very least, it is a wonder that the Rhenish gold guilders are so rare today, since, as Freig calculates, they were once minted so frequently.

-> §. 8. <-
