<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of historical anecdotes regarding Rhenish gold to the author's personal evaluation of metallic transmutation. The author shifts from citing others to proposing his own empirical investigations into the nature of metals.</meta>

enough has been said regarding the opinion of the **chemists** <note>original: *Chymisten*; here referring to alchemists and early chemical theorists.</note>. To this we must add our own experiments; we also wish to consult the **metallurgists** <note>Those who specialize in the extraction and processing of metals.</note>, look into the **ore mines** if possible—especially those in which nobler metals <note>Gold and silver.</note> are produced—and consult the literature, even though I prefer the evidence of my own eyes; only then can we judge the **transformation of metals** <term>transmutation</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing one metal into another, typically base metals into gold or silver.</note> more thoroughly. However, so that I may establish something certain regarding the transformation of metals—whether it can occur through human diligence or not—I certainly maintain that it is possible, and indeed, that it has actually happened.

But I do not wish to rehash what has been said a hundred times. How many treatises, how many books have been written on this subject, in which the proofs for and against the matter are presented? I do not wish to abuse your patience, my Lord, by repeating them. I set aside all useless questions: “Whether metals are alive? Whether they have a vegetative soul or a seed? Whether they differ from one another in their essential nature or only in **accidental properties** <term>accidents</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are qualities (like color or hardness) that do not change the underlying essence of a thing.</note>? Whether one species can be transformed into another?” All these things are mostly the monstrous offspring of wit. That metals are alive, **Berigard** <note>Claude Guillermet de Bérigard (1592–1663), a French philosopher whose work *Circulus Pisanus* (The Pisan Circle) examined Aristotelian and Epicurean philosophy.</note> attempted to demonstrate in a plausible treatise in his *Circulus Pisanus*, Book 8, as did **Giordano Bruno** <note>Giordano Bruno (1548–1600), the Italian philosopher and cosmologist; the reference likely points to his work *De la causa, principio et uno*.</note> in his 5th Book.

<meta>On the previous page, the author began exploring whether metals possess a form of "life" or "vegetation" and expressed a desire to move past abstract philosophical debates to focus on empirical evidence regarding the transmutation of metals.</meta>

In the work *Concerning the Universe and the Innumerable* <note>original Latin title: *de universo et innumerabilibus*</note>, Chapter 12, it is claimed that the entire Earth and all its parts possess a life of their own, which the author intends to prove by the example of stones. He believes they are nourished in the same manner as teeth in the human body. Others deny this, particularly those who follow **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher whose theories on the four elements dominated medieval and early modern science.</note>, such as Scaliger, Caesalpinus, Jacob Aubert in his book *On the Origin of Metals* <note>original: *de Ortu metallorum*</note>, and Fallopius. Some attribute a **seed** <term>semen metallorum</term> <note>The alchemical belief that metals grow from seeds within the earth, much like plants.</note> to metals, while others deny it. There is also much quarreling over the **essential and accidental differences** <term>essentia et accidentia</term> <note>A philosophical debate over whether metals are fundamentally different substances or the same substance with different outward qualities.</note> of metals, with which I do not wish to fill this paper, especially since nothing significant depends upon it.

Furthermore, for someone who might stubbornly deny the **vegetation of metals** <note>The theory that metals "grow" or sprout within the earth or through chemical reactions.</note>, such a process could be demonstrated through the well-known **Diana’s Tree** <term>Arbor Dianæ</term> <note>A famous chemical experiment where silver crystals grow out of a mercury solution in a tree-like structure, often cited by alchemists as proof that metals could "grow."</note> made from mercury, as well as the wonderful germination of the same in "mercurial water." This latter experiment was performed by Saint-Simon in Brussels using a powder he received from an unknown man, as Borrichius reports in the book *On the Origin and Progress of Chemistry* <note>original: *de Ortu et progr. Chem.*</note>, page 103. From this, one must conclude that there was a silver seed, or something resembling a seed, within that powder.

It is not my intention to treat the transformations of metals in general, for among the **imperfect metals** <note>Base metals like lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists believed could be "perfected" into gold or silver.</note> there are all kinds

<meta>This page continues the rebuttal against the Jesuit Honoratus Fabri. The previous page introduced Fabri’s skepticism regarding the "Artist’s" (alchemist's) ability to alter or destroy the perfect nature of gold.</meta>

->※ ❁ ※<-

...is consumed; indeed, that it could only be thinned and worn away by a vapor within the lids of dishes <note>This likely refers to the wear and tear of gilded or gold-plated cookware, or perhaps a specific chemical observation of the time regarding how gold surfaces interact with heat and moisture.</note> cannot be admitted. Gold, as he asserts—and indeed rightly—consists of a twofold composition of parts: an internal and an external one. The internal is that which consists of the primordial and mixed parts; the external contains the mixed parts of the gold. Many things can destroy the external cohesion, such as rubbing, striking, or salts, such that it passes away in imperceptible particles, each of which nevertheless retains the form of gold. One cannot say in this regard, as Fabri wishes, that the gold is destroyed simply because these tangible parts cannot be reconstituted into gold. The internal composition, however, can be freed from its bonds by nothing except that which originates from the primordial nature of gold itself. The principle which Fabri thus establishes: “If gold could be destroyed in no way, it would not go so poorly for the chemists <note>In this context, “Chemists” refers to alchemists attempting transmutation.</note>,” I express with more right thus: “If the chemists could completely destroy gold, it would go well for them <note>In alchemy, the "destruction" of a metal—reducing it to its "prime matter"—was the essential first step (the *nigredo* or blackening) before it could be transformed or "exalted."</note>.” Still others come parading their “species” and “forms”—those magnificent names—and boldly claim that such things cannot be changed. Nevertheless, they fail to notice that this is the daily business of Nature,

Mercury, tin, silver, and lead are far lighter than the others which originate from red sulfur. For their sulfur—as seen in iron and copper—has a greater impurity linked with a certain power than is found in those where <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurius*; in alchemy, this refers to the "spirit" or principle of fluidity and metallic luster rather than just the liquid metal.</note> is more abundant, and where the impure sulfur can be more easily boiled out from the perfect sulfur into a red state. For it is also brought into an imperfect red state through common processing. Therefore, in the work of transformation, <term>chemists</term> <note>In the 17th and 18th centuries, the terms "chemist" and "alchemist" were often used interchangeably.</note> prefer to use lead or mercury rather than iron or copper.

When they make their **elixir**, they unite their processed, purified metallic matter with the purest **gold-sulfur**, and convert it into a nature that is most subtle and yet most fixed. This substance is far more perfect than gold itself, for through repeated dissolving and <term>coagulating</term> <note>original: *coaguliren*; the process of thickening or solidifying a substance, often through cooling or evaporation.</note> it receives ever higher degrees of perfection.

In contrast, there can be a liquid <note>original: *Liquor*; in this context, a concentrated medicinal or chemical solution.</note> which, with just a few drops, immediately restores the most spoiled wine and sour beer to its former and better state. "The use of aqua vitae <note>original: *Aquavit*, literally 'water of life,' referring here to distilled spirits or brandy.</note> first became known from certain books of Arabian physicians, who prepared it for the art of medicine. It remained as such until the Modenese <note>inhabitants of Modena, Italy</note> sent it in great quantities to the northern countries, and thereby caused it to be counted among ordinary beverages, which happened in this way. At first, aqua vitae was made only from spoiled wines, and in such small amounts that one could hardly drink a drop. However, there was once so much wine grown throughout all of Italy that a good portion of the Modenese wine sold in Venice—which was weaker than the rest and thus found no buyers—spoiled. The Modenese distilled aqua vitae from it, into which they had mixed the wine that was not yet spoiled but which they nevertheless could not sell; they thus made aqua vitae in greater quantities, which they sold to the Venetians, twice-distilled, at a profit. When the Venetians heard that the miners in Germany required such a drink, which would maintain the strength and heat of the body, they sold..."

We do not yet have an exact history of the **parting artists** <term>parting artists (Scheidekünstler): early chemists or alchemists specializing in the separation of metals and the study of chemical properties</term>, as we do for other sciences; perhaps we shall never obtain one, because the art, along with its teachers, is covered in great darkness, and they have hidden themselves as much as possible. ...logically arranged <note>original: *nologice concinnatam*, completing the word "chronologically" from the previous page</note> with a second part and brief biographies of the authors; a *Book on the History of Chemical Projections* <note>original: *Librum de proiectionum chimicarum historiis*</note>; a *French Chemical Theater* <note>original: *Theatrum chimicum gallicum*</note>; a *Dissertation on Whether the **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>philosopher's stone (lapis philosophorum): the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life</term> Should be Made Public, and on the Revelation of its Matter*; and seven folio volumes *On the Matter and Properties of Minerals* <note>original: *de materia et proprietatibus mineralium*</note>, etc. Until now, nothing of these has been seen except for the titles. Perhaps someone else might execute more precisely what he promised.

For I know a nobleman who not only possesses fine and extraordinary **chemical secrets** <term>chemical secrets (chymische Geheimnisse): hidden recipes or processes, often concerning the transmutation of metals or the creation of medicinal elixirs</term>, but has also, with unbelievable diligence and many years of labor, brought together and compared all chemical authors he could record, both ancient and modern. He has sought out their history from the darkness as much as possible, so that I doubt whether any scholar has ever undertaken or will ever undertake such work. Perhaps **Johann Gerhard Vossius** <note>Gerhard Johann Vossius (1577–1649), a Dutch classical scholar and theologian whose works organized many fields of knowledge.</note> would have told us rare things in his work *On the Constitution of All Arts and Sciences* <note>original: *de constitutione omnium artium et scientiarum*</note>, where he promised to treat the **art of gold-making** <term>art of gold-making (Goldmacherkunst): chrysopoeia, the branch of alchemy specifically focused on the creation of gold</term>, in his *Gentile Theology* <note>original: *Theol. gent.*, referring to *De theologia gentili*</note> Book 2, Chapter 6, Rule 5. However, this was either left unprinted or unfinished by him, as was also the case with his book on **philology** and the **mathematical sciences**.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of chemistry, moving from the bibliographical efforts mentioned on the previous page to a specific evaluation of the most important chemical authors, starting with the foundational figure Geber.</meta>

->※ ❁ ※<-

### §. 12.

Since there is such a great number of chemical writers and even more impostors—distinguishing between whom requires great discernment—we will mention only the most prominent among them. **Geber** <note>Jābir ibn Hayyān (c. 721–815), the most influential figure in Islamic and medieval European alchemy. His works formed the basis of Western chemical theory.</note>, an Arab who is said to have been a prince, is counted among the oldest authors after the birth of Christ. We have some writings by him that are full of digressions and ambiguities. Scholars judge him thus: he was the greatest philosopher and the greatest sophist <term>sophist: in this historical context, someone who uses intentionally obscure or deceptive language to hide profound truths</term>; for in his works, he means something entirely different by even the most common words. His writings have been much altered, and **Georg Horn** <note>Georg Horn (1620–1670), a German historian and professor at Leiden known for his works on the history of philosophy.</note> recently published some in Holland that differ from those that first appeared. **Christian Rau** <note>Christian Raue (1613–1677), a prominent Orientalist and linguist who collected many Eastern manuscripts.</note> brought back from the Orient a manuscript <note>original: *Codicem*</note> of Geber's writings which is half as large again as his *Spoils of the Orient* <note>original: *Spolium Orientis*, a catalog of Eastern manuscripts published in Kiel in 1666</note> shows. His oldest commentator is **Paganus**, whose writings have not appeared until now, for they remain confined within the collections of enthusiasts <note>original: *Schranken der Liebhaber*, referring to private circles of alchemy collectors who have not yet published the manuscripts</note>. He writes more clearly than anyone before him. As far as can be seen, he was a Frenchman, and those are mistaken who consider him and Geber to be one and the same; for they believe **Lull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher to whom many later alchemical works were attributed.</note> gave him this name because he was a Muslim <note>original: *Mahomedaner*</note>.

| chemical writers | Geber | Georg Horn | Christian Rau |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| *Spoils of the Orient* | Paganus | Lull | Muslim |

<summary>This page evaluates the works of Geber, the famous Arabic alchemist, noting the difficulty of interpreting his obscure language and the existence of various manuscript versions. It also introduces the commentator Paganus, clarifying that he is a distinct French author rather than being identical to Geber.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, alchemy authors, Georg Horn, Christian Rau, Paganus, Ramon Llull, chemical manuscripts, Spolium Orientis, history of chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the suppression of alchemical works by Church historians. ...to have read **Llull** and **Villanova**, but the book is not at hand. The papal scribes are also in the habit of keeping silent about the **chemical writings** <term>chemical writings: at this time, chemistry and alchemy were often used interchangeably to refer to the study of matter and transmutation</term> of their own people, or regarding them as forged, even though it is certain that they were written by them. This has also been done by them when citing the writings of **Villanova** and **Llull**; they exclude an entire volume of chemical treatises as false and forged from his works—material that exists by **Villanova** in a separate part under the title: *Chemical Writings* <note>Original Latin: "Chymica Scripta"</note>.

Among other English writers, **Pits** <note>John Pits (1560–1616), a Catholic biographer who wrote 'De illustribus Angliae scriptoribus'.</note>, who collected the *Centuries* of **Bale** <note>John Bale (1495–1563), a historian and controversialist whose bibliography of British authors was divided into "centuries" or groups of one hundred.</note>, has done this. For whenever he makes mention of chemical writings written by famous men—whether from the writings of **Leland** <note>John Leland (c. 1503–1552), an antiquary who traveled England searching for documents in monastic libraries.</note> or from another account—this copyist **Pits** hides everything with a stubborn silence, so that no memory of such matters remains. This was perhaps done out of contempt for the art of **making gold**, so that it might not seem as if they had devoted any effort to a matter forbidden by papal decrees <note>Referring specifically to the 1317 decree 'Spondet quas non exhibent' by Pope John XXII, which targeted alchemists for fraud.</note> or deemed unworthy of them. It is a constant tradition, or as **Camden** <note>William Camden (1551–1623), an English historian and antiquarian.</note> in his *Remains* <note>Original: "Remaines," referring to Camden's 'Remaines Concerning Britaine' (1605).</note>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the reputation of Ramon Llull and the historical evidence for his alchemical activities. -> ✠ ❁ ✠ <-

Page 172 says, an "unwritten truth" <note>original: *by an unwritten Verity*</note> (aside from what **Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher who, in alchemical legend, was said to have been recruited by the Abbot of Westminster to make gold for the English crown.</note> himself taught here and there, and **Cremer** <note>German: *Cramer*. Referring to John Cremer, the semi-legendary Abbot of Westminster who is often featured in alchemical lore as Llull's companion.</note>, the Abbot of **Westminster**, testifies in his testament): that **Llull** made gold for **King Edward III** in the Tower of London. The King used this gold against the heathens, and from it a coin was struck which was called the **Rose Noble** or the **Noble of Raymond** <note>original: *Nobile Raimundi*</note>. **Selden** <note>John Selden (1584–1654), a renowned English jurist and scholar.</note> gives an illustration of this in the second book, page 25, of *The Closed Sea* <note>original: *Maris clausi*</note>. On one side there is a rose, and on the other the chemical symbol: *But Jesus passed through the midst of them* <note>original Latin: *Jesus autem transiens per medium illorum ibat*. This verse from Luke 4:30 was inscribed on the Rose Noble; alchemists interpreted it as a metaphor for the elusive nature of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

**Robert Constantin** <note>A 16th-century French scholar and physician.</note> testifies in his *Nomenclature of Medical Writers* <note>original: *Nomencl. script. med.*</note> that he investigated the matter and found it to be true. Thus, it is a false claim by **Alexander von Suchten** <note>A Paracelsian chemist (c. 1520–1575) who was skeptical of many alchemical traditions.</note>, who wants to make us believe that the "Nobles of Raymond" came from a "King Raymond"—though no such king ever existed in England—and not from **Ramon Llull**.

This is further strengthened because **Camden** <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a famous English antiquarian and historian.</note> himself notes in the cited passage: that before the time of **Edward III**, no gold coins were struck in England. Perhaps this **making of gold** <term>making of gold: also known as *Goldmacherey* or transmutation, the alchemical process of turning base metals into gold.</term> also gave **Edward** the occasion to pass a law stating that no gold or silver should be taken out of the country, and also that it should not be multiplied <note>This likely refers to the "Act Against Multipliers" (1404), which prohibited the alchemical "multiplication" of gold and silver.</note>;

...which a certain English scholar understands as if all alchemy were forbidden under the term "multiplication" <note>The "multiplication" of gold and silver refers to an alchemical process, but also to the legal concern of "multiplying" precious metals by debasing them with cheaper ones.</note> of gold and silver. However, this is an error. For the meaning of the law (which is written in the English language and can be found in Pulton’s statute book <note>Ferdinando Pulton (1536–1618) was an English jurist famous for compiling *A Collection of Sundry Statutes*.</note>) is that one should not mix other metals with gold and silver, for that is what is understood by "multiplication," as the words of the law itself suggest. A prohibition of chemical multiplication <note>The actual creation of gold through alchemy.</note> would also have been foolish. It is also quite fitting to mention here that Henry IV <note>King of England (r. 1399–1413). He famously repealed some restrictions while attempting to use alchemy to solve the Crown's debts.</note> was moved by this history to issue four edicts, in which he exhorted all noblemen, soldiers, doctors, professors, and priests to discover the preparation of the **Philosopher’s Stone**, and everyone was to reveal it so that the realm might be freed from debt. For the priests, he used this ridiculous reasoning: that they could very easily transform a base metal into a nobler one, since they were already so successful in transforming bread and wine into the body and blood of Christ <note>A skeptical reference to the Catholic doctrine of Transubstantiation, suggesting that if a priest can change the substance of bread into God, changing lead to gold should be simple.</note>. But it was in vain, and no one came forward. John Pettus <note>Sir John Pettus (1613–1690), an English politician and author interested in metallurgy.</note> cites these edicts in the English book: *Royal Mines* <note>original: *Fodinae Regales, or the History, Laws, and Places of the Chief Mines and Mineral Works in England*</note>, Part 1, Chapter 27. And I have myself [consulted] with the royal archivist...

It also recounts King Henry IV's failed attempt to use alchemical gold—humorously compared to transubstantiation—to pay off national debts.</summary>

...inquired into the matter himself, for he still kept the original manuscript in the archives. Nevertheless, there has been no shortage of those who, despite all these credible testimonies, have nonetheless doubted the story of Lulli <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic. A massive body of alchemical writing was later attributed to him, though modern scholars consider these "pseudo-Lullian" works written by others.</note> and his **art of gold-making**, as they were led to do so by the chronological uncertainty of those times. 

For—to pass over the most well-known facts in silence—Vincentius Mutius <note>Vicenç Mut (1614–1687), a Spanish historian and astronomer.</note>, a Spaniard, sought in his book *History of the Kingdom of Majorca* <note>Original: *Historia del regno de Malorca*.</note> to undermine everything said about Lulli’s alchemy through various arguments. By attributing the story to the earlier Edward, Borrichius attempts to resolve the "impossible" timeline of Llull and the King meeting.</note>. However, since Vincentius Mutius was entirely inexperienced in English history, as Borrichius has already proven, it is probable that he is also mistaken regarding this Lullian history. 

If we believe Mutius, Lulli was born in 1235. Edward III ascended to the throne in 1327 in the 14th year of his age, which would be the 11th year after Lulli's death. In the first year of his reign, he <insert>confirmed</insert> the University of Cambridge...

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the works of Petrus Bonus (Pietro Bono) and transitions into a review of Roger Bacon's scientific and alchemical contributions, following the previous page's introduction to Bono’s "Precious Pearl."</meta>

...[already] published in Basel in 1572, which edition is better than the one found in the **Chemical Theater** <note>original: *Theatro chymico*; a massive and influential compendium of alchemical texts first published in the early 17th century.</note>. He also wrote, as he says at the end of the book, another treatise on this subject, which he preferred to suppress rather than bring to light. A short extract from it, and from other such writings, was provided by **Janus Lacinius**, a Calabrian monk, in the **Chemical Collections** <note>original: *Collectaneis chymicis*</note>, which was printed in Nuremberg by **Petreius** <note>Johann Petreius (c. 1497–1550), a renowned Nuremberg printer famous for publishing scientific works, including Copernicus.</note>; it also appears in the **Precious Pearl** <note>original: *Pretiosa Margarita*</note>, which was published in Venice by **Aldus** <note>The Aldine Press, founded by Aldus Manutius, known for its exquisite typography and the first use of italic type.</note> in a beautiful printing with copperplate engravings of chemical vessels.

The writings of the Englishman **Roger Bacon** are likewise, and indeed rightly, esteemed by lovers of chemistry. A few are found in the **Chemical Theater**; some have also been published separately in Frankfurt. His manuscripts are tucked away in English libraries, and whatever can be acquired of his work is paid for with a great deal of money. He was, far beyond the standards of his century, astonishingly learned in all branches of the sciences. Whoever reads his **Letter on the Secrets of Nature and Art** <note>original: *Brief von den Geheimnissen der Natur und Kunst*; referring to the *Epistola de secretis operibus artis et naturae*, where Bacon discusses potential technologies like flying machines and steamships.</note> will find therein many inventions in physical and mathematical matters of which our own modern times are quite proud. At the very least, the invention of gunpowder <note>original: *Pulver*</note> is to be attributed to him and not to **Berthold Schwarz**, a Norman <note>Berthold Schwarz was a legendary German friar often credited with inventing gunpowder, though historical evidence suggests Bacon described the formula much earlier.</note>, since he

Flamel’s [riches] were infinite, but he dispensed them with a generous hand to the poor, to churches, and to hospitals, so that in Paris there is almost no hospital or church where his portrait and chemical hieroglyphs <note>Symbolic alchemical imagery or emblems often found on the monuments Flamel endowed, such as those at the Cemetery of the Innocents.</note> are not found. There was a certain Perier, a physician and owner of this powder, from whom his cousin Bois stole it from his papers after his death—though to his own misfortune. <meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of the anonymous alchemist known as Philaletha (Eirenaeus Philalethes), following the mention of the bibliographer Pierre Borel. It details the transmission of Philaletha's manuscripts through George Starkey.</meta>

[...as Borellus, in his *Library*, notes that] in London, a distinguished and credible man who knows him well has told the following. He once landed in the East Indies at a place whose name has slipped my mind, and was hosted by his countryman, **George Starkey**, an apothecary. He lived on very intimate terms with Starkey’s son, and revealed his name to him under an oath; he also gave him some of the <term>tincture</term> <note>original: *Tincur*; the elusive substance, also called the Philosopher's Stone, capable of transmuting metals and curing diseases.</note>, along with a book intended for publication. A portion of this was published a few years ago in Amsterdam by **Johann Lange** under the title: *Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King* <note>original: *Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium*; first published in 1667, this became one of the most influential alchemical manuals in Europe.</note>; the other part, however, was brought to light under a different title by the **Physician Birr** in Amsterdam, to whom the name **Philaletha** <note>A pseudonym meaning "peaceful lover of truth." While the text treats Philaletha and Starkey as separate people, modern scholarship generally identifies George Starkey himself as the author behind the Philaletha persona.</note> was unknown. The book circulated for a long time in manuscript form among friends of chemistry, and **Georg Horn** <note>Georg Horn (1620–1670) was a German historian and professor at Leiden who took a keen interest in alchemical history.</note> mentions it in the preface to **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan; Horn likely refers to his 1668 edition of the *Gebri Arabis Philosophi Sollertissimi*.</note>; however, he is unfair toward this excellent writer—who is more sincere and clearer than almost any other—and calls him a <term>sophist</term> <note>In the context of alchemy, a "sophist" was a derogatory term for a "puffer" or a fraud who lacked true philosophical understanding.</note> simply because he achieved no results by following his instructions. It is highly probable that the book was written in Latin; for I cannot [concur with] the opinion of the one who recently published the original from the English—which differs somewhat from the Lange edition—...

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic survey of alchemical literature from page 119. The previous page ended while listing major compendiums like the *Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum*. ...to philosophize, not to stumble. As long as one understands the subject matter, a fair-minded reader will easily forgive the choice of words and expressions. There were sometimes brilliant minds hidden in monasteries who, through their leisure and lack of worldly worries regarding sustenance, were able to fathom many secrets through diligent and lengthy contemplation—secrets that others, burdened by more business, do not reach. For this reason, we find more traces of chemistry <term>Chymie</term> <note>original: *Chymie*; here referring to the "sacred art" of alchemy and early chemical experimentation.</note> among the monks than among others. 

But whoever wishes to derive benefit from their writings must understand a special art of interpretation <term>Erklärungs-Kunst</term> <note>original: *Erklärungs-Kunst*; the specific skill of decoding allegorical or "deckname" (cover-name) language used by alchemists.</note>. He must know their hidden technical terms <term>Kunstwörter</term>, of which one could compile an entire lexicon; for there are plenty of dictionaries written for common words. In this case, one must seek the meaning by comparing similar passages, of which Lagneau <note>David Lagneau (c. 1560–1644), a French physician who sought to harmonize the often contradictory statements of various alchemists.</note> provided a sample in his *Chemical Harmony* <term>Harmonia chymica</term> <note>original: *Harmonia chymica*.</note>. In this very way—though through hurried labor—the *Assembly of Philosophers* <term>Turba philosophorum</term> <note>original: *Turba philosophorum*; one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, which takes the form of a debate between pre-Socratic philosophers.</note> was compiled, which is, however, quite different from the one found in the *Chemical Theater* <term>Theatro chymico</term> <note>original: *Theatrum Chemicum*; the massive multi-volume compendium of alchemical literature.</note>. Count Bernard of Treviso <note>Bernhardus Trevisanus (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist who allegedly spent his life and fortune seeking the Stone, eventually finding it in old age.</note> produced another, also in the French language.

It remains for me to [record] one thing and another previously unknown, which has reached human knowledge from the experiments of this art

...to philosophize, not to bicker over terms <note>original: *stoffen*; in this context, it refers to quibbling over word choices or linguistic stumbling blocks rather than focusing on the underlying philosophy.</note>. But whoever wishes to derive benefit from their writings must understand a special art of interpretation. He must know their hidden technical terms <term>Kunstwörter</term>, of which one could create an entire lexicon. For there are plenty of dictionaries written for common words. Here, one must seek the meaning by comparing similar passages against one another, of which **Lagnäus** <note>David Lagneau (c. 1570–1644), a French physician whose *Harmonia chymica* (1611) attempted to reconcile the diverse and often contradictory statements of various alchemists.</note> has provided a sample in his *Chemical Harmony* <note>original: *Harmonia chymica*.</note>. [Eng]land showed various proofs of his art, such that he nearly brought about his own ruin thereby. The golden part was entirely porous, which demonstrates the power of the tincture <term>Tinctur</term> <note>Also known as the "red tincture" or Philosopher's Stone, believed to be a concentrated substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> upon the particles of the metal. The Lord of Noyers, who possessed this Thaler <term>Thaler</term> <note>A large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries.</note>, displayed it often in Paris. I am acquainted with a friend who saw a similar Thaler belonging to him featuring golden and silver stripes. **Sendivogius** performed the tincture upon it in the following manner: with a brush dipped in water, he drew lines upon the Thaler; upon these lines he strewed the powder, and placed the coin into the fire until it glowed. Where the lines had been drawn, it turned to gold, while the rest remained silver. However, this could not occur without a great loss of the tincture, of which one must utilize a greater amount upon glowing metal than upon molten metal. In exactly the same way, iron nails can be tinctured, where the quantity of the tincture must compensate for what the material lacks; just as petrifying juices transform wood and similar materials into stone, being present in such quantity that they are able to fill all the pores and passages.

There, he found by the window a book in the old Welsh language (which he understood well) written about the Philosopher's Stone; upon reading it through, he suspected it contained matters of great importance. When he asked where the innkeeper had obtained it, the man replied that it had been found in the grave of a bishop in the nearby church. The matter occurred thus: When the mob <note>Likely referring to the iconoclasm during the Reformation.</note> stormed the images and crosses of the church, they also opened the bishop's grave in the hope of finding great treasures

...[Sendi]vogius <note>original: "sendus". This refers to the famous Polish alchemist Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636).</note> relates in his book *On Metals*, Chapter 7 <note>original: "Libr. de metall. K. 7."</note>. It is commonly believed that he [Kelly] was the owner of the secret, but in truth, he had obtained the tincture <term>Tincture</term> <note>A concentrated alchemical substance believed to be capable of transmuting metals.</note> from another. However, since his story is not fully known, I will recount it as I heard it from the mouth of a nobleman who had it from the servant of **Kelly** <note>original: "Kelläus". Edward Kelly (1555–1597) was an English spirit medium and alchemist.</note>. It is a tale full of curiosities.

**Kelly** was no English nobleman, as some claim, but a notary and advocate in **London**, of common birth. Because he was skilled in the Old English language, he took it upon himself, for the sake of pleasing others, to forge certain old documents. When he was convicted of this crime, according to the testimony of **Weever** in *Ancient Funeral Monuments* <note>original: "Weaver in Monumentis funeralibus". This refers to the antiquarian John Weever's 1631 work.</note>, he was banished from **London** after having his ears cut off <note>Ear-cropping was a standard legal punishment for forgers and counterfeiters in Tudor England.</note>.

With this, he traveled to the province of **Wales**, and stayed with the innkeeper of a small town whose name has escaped me. There, by the window, he found a book written in the old Welsh language—which he understood well—concerning the **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>The legendary "Great Work" of alchemy, a substance that could turn lead into gold and provide the Elixir of Life.</note>. Upon reading through it, he suspected it contained important matters. When he asked where the innkeeper had obtained it, the man replied that it had been found in the grave of a bishop in the nearby church.

This is how the matter occurred: when the mob stormed the images and crosses of the church <note>This refers to the iconoclasm of the Reformation, where religious symbols and tombs were often destroyed or looted.</note>, they also opened the bishop's grave in the hope of finding great treasures

Those who did not know that he had obtained the tincture from elsewhere believed he was able to transform as much base metal into gold as the gold weighed from which he had perhaps made a powder or a liquor <term>Liquor</term> <note>In alchemy, a liquid substance or solution used in the process of transmutation.</note>. Presumably, he did not have as much tincture as was necessary to tincture <term>Tincture</term> <note>To "tincture" means to dye or transform the nature of a metal, here referring to the act of transmutation.</note> by the pound.

"I will faithfully tell you," he says, "what once happened to me when I was in great doubt whether mercury <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note> could be transformed into gold. A diligent man, who wished to remove this doubt from me, gave me one drachm <term>Drachma</term> <note>A unit of weight roughly equal to 3.9 grams.</note> of a powder that had the color of the blossom of a field poppy <note>The deep red color of the poppy is significant in alchemy, as it corresponds to the 'rubedo' or red stage, indicating the final perfection of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, but a taste like burnt sea salt. To avoid all the annoyance of a playful fraud, he bought vessels from many others, as well as mercury and coals, in which no gold could be hidden, as often happens with fraudsters. Upon ten drachms of the same [mercury], over a strong fire, I threw my powder, which immediately—without much loss—was transformed into nearly ten drachms of gold, which, according to the judgment of the goldsmiths, passed all tests. If I had not done this in a secluded place, without witnesses, I might suspect a fraud; for I can sincerely testify that the matter happened thus."

So far this learned and honest man. To this I add what <term>Du Hamel</term> <note>Jean-Baptiste Du Hamel (1624–1706), a French scientist and the first secretary of the Royal Academy of Sciences.</note> relates in *On Fossils* <note>original: "de fossilibus" – In the 17th century, 'fossils' referred to anything dug out of the earth, including minerals and metals.</note> Book 2, Chapter 10, Page 5. "A few years ago," he says (the book was published in 1660), "a goldsmith in Paris, whose name I could mention if it were necessary, received several grains of the gold-making powder from a Pole who was traveling back to his fatherland, through which...

I could add much more to all of these which has been reported to me by credible friends. However, I keep this only for myself

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical testimonies from the previous page, shifting from specific historical figures like Martini and Monconys to a broader philosophical defense of the "Art" against its skeptics and the social risks involved in its study.</meta>

...and some good friends, and may not say it publicly. This may already be enough to combat the stubbornness of those who allow themselves to be convinced by no reasons and hardly even by their own senses, as indeed most people are. For since the transformations and the hidden power of nature are unknown to the greater part of them—or because each person measures such things according to his own narrow intentions—it tends to happen that he initially casts doubt on everything that appears new and unusual to him. Afterwards, because he cannot comprehend it, he despises it as empty and fictional, and laughs at those who investigate these matters out of a thirst for knowledge. 

Just as that pride and malice are to be blamed, so this diligence is to be approved; yet, it is not to be advised thoughtlessly. For because this science <note>The German word "Wissenschaft" here refers to a systematic branch of knowledge or study, in this case, alchemy.</note> is hedged about with so many difficulties, he does not act wisely who spends money and precious time on an uncertain matter. We shall apply all our efforts in vain, unless as it were a <term>providential intervention</term> <note>original: "Deus ex machina"; a Latin term referring to a sudden, unexpected solution to a difficulty, or a divine revelation.</note> or a good teacher comes to us. 

To this is added the unfortunate contempt that accompanies this Art <note>The "Art" is a standard historical term for alchemy.</note>, which is difficult for an honest man to bear. For our unsuccessfully spent efforts take away much of our good reputation among upright people, because we undertake a futile cause that is stained by so many frauds, and made notorious through loss and danger—

<summary>This page reflects on the public skepticism and social dangers surrounding alchemy, arguing that while nature's hidden powers are real, the pursuit of the "Art" is so difficult and prone to fraud that it risks a man's fortune and reputation.</summary>
<keywords>skepticism, nature's power, scientific diligence, Deus ex machina, reputation, alchemical fraud, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page concludes a letter discussing the social and intellectual risks of alchemy. The author, likely a legal scholar or humanist, contrasts the "steep heights" of speculative alchemy and astrology with the practical benefits of natural philosophy and medicine, ultimately deferring to the recipient's expertise.</meta>

...notorious. Therefore, Cardinal **Perron** <note>Jacques Davy Duperron (1556–1618), a French cardinal and diplomat known for his vast library and intellectual pursuits.</note>, as is clear from his papers (which the **Patin** brothers <note>Refers to the family of Guy Patin, a famous French physician and man of letters whose sons were also scholars.</note> collected), counts those among the foolish minds who spend their time on these matters, or on the doubling of the cube <note>original: "Multiplication des Cubus"; a famous mathematical problem from antiquity involving the construction of a cube with twice the volume of a given cube using only a compass and straightedge.</note>, or on perpetual motion, or on astrology. Thus, those do better who leave these steep heights and walk on level roads, observing the immediate effects of nature with less harm and danger, from which a new light is always kindled for natural philosophy and the art of medicine. You, my dear sir, do this with the approval of all the learned, who constantly look forward to new secrets from your chemical workshop. I, who have dedicated myself to quite different endeavors (for what fellowship do Roman laws or the liberal arts have with chemistry?), see the matter as if through a telescope and leave it to your judgment. If this letter, which is already far too long, is not worthy of seeing the light of day, then you may throw it into the fire and sacrifice it to Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire; here used as a literary metaphor for burning the manuscript.</note>. Farewell. Kiel, the 26th of February 1673.

<summary>The author concludes his letter by advising against speculative "foolish" sciences like alchemy and astrology, favoring practical medicine and natural study. He deferentially submits his thoughts to the recipient, a chemist, and dates the letter from Kiel in 1673.</summary>
